# Biology — Questions

Answers: [answers.md](answers.md). Question numbers match answer numbers.

## VCE Units 1 & 2

### Unit 1 AOS 1: How do cells function?

#### Cell theory and cell size

**Q1.** Which statement is a principle of cell theory?
<!-- id: bio12a1-ct-1 -->

- A. All cells contain a nucleus.
- B. All cells arise from pre-existing cells.
- C. All living things are made of many cells.
- D. Cells can arise spontaneously from non-living matter.

**Q2.** A cube-shaped cell has a side length of 2 µm. What is its surface area to volume ratio?
<!-- id: bio12a1-ct-2 -->

- A. 1:1
- B. 2:1
- C. 3:1
- D. 6:1

**Q3.** As a cell grows larger while keeping the same shape, which change occurs?
<!-- id: bio12a1-ct-3 -->

- A. Its volume increases more slowly than its surface area.
- B. Its SA:V ratio increases.
- C. Its rate of diffusion across each square micrometre of membrane decreases.
- D. Its SA:V ratio decreases, so exchange per unit volume becomes less efficient.

**Q4.** Agar cubes of three sizes (1 cm, 2 cm and 3 cm sides) containing a pH indicator were placed in acid for 5 minutes. Which result would be expected?
<!-- id: bio12a1-ct-4 -->

- A. The 3 cm cube would show the greatest percentage of its volume changed colour.
- B. All cubes would show the same percentage of volume changed colour.
- C. The 1 cm cube would show the greatest percentage of its volume changed colour.
- D. The acid would penetrate a greater distance into the 3 cm cube.

**Q5.** Which feature helps a large cell overcome the limits of a low SA:V ratio?
<!-- id: bio12a1-ct-5 -->

- A. A spherical shape
- B. Microvilli or a flattened, elongated shape
- C. A thicker cell wall
- D. Fewer membrane transport proteins

<!-- id: bio12a1-ct-6 -->

*Written answer*

**Q9.** In Louis Pasteur's swan-neck flask experiment, broth in a flask with a long curved neck stayed free of microbial growth while the neck remained intact. What did this result support?
<!-- id: bio12a1-ct-9 -->

- A. Microorganisms arise spontaneously from the nutrient broth itself.
- B. Microorganisms need light to grow in nutrient broth.
- C. Microorganisms in the broth came from pre-existing microorganisms carried into it from the air.
- D. Heating the broth creates new microorganisms within it.

**Q10.** Which cube-shaped cell has the highest surface area to volume ratio?
<!-- id: bio12a1-ct-10 -->

- A. A cube with a side length of 1 µm
- B. A cube with a side length of 5 µm
- C. A cube with a side length of 10 µm
- D. A cube with a side length of 20 µm

**Q11.** A student claims that doubling the side length of a cube-shaped cell doubles its surface area to volume ratio. Which statement correctly evaluates this claim?
<!-- id: bio12a1-ct-11 -->

- A. The claim is correct, because the surface area and the volume both double.
- B. The claim is incorrect, because the SA:V ratio halves when the side length doubles.
- C. The claim is incorrect, but only because the shape of the cell has changed.
- D. The claim is incorrect, because the SA:V ratio is multiplied by four when the side length doubles.

**Q12.** A cube-shaped cell has a surface area of 54 µm². What is its surface area to volume ratio?
<!-- id: bio12a1-ct-12 -->

- A. 6:1
- B. 1:2
- C. 3:1
- D. 2:1

**Q13.** A cube-shaped cell has a volume of 0.125 µm³. What is its surface area to volume ratio?
<!-- id: bio12a1-ct-13 -->

- A. 12:1
- B. 24:1
- C. 1:12
- D. 6:1

**Q14.** A cube-shaped cell grows in side length from 2 µm to 6 µm while keeping its shape. By what factor does its volume increase?
<!-- id: bio12a1-ct-14 -->

- A. 3
- B. 27
- C. 9
- D. 81

- A. 100 nm
- B. 1 mm

- D. 10 cm

<!-- id: bio12a1-ct-16 -->

- A. 2 µm
- B. 200 µm
- C. 0.02 µm
- D. 20 µm

<!-- id: bio12a1-ct-17 -->

- A. 4.2:1
- B. 6:1

**Q21.** A single cube of side 2 µm and eight separate cubes of side 1 µm have the same total volume. Which statement is correct?
<!-- id: bio12a1-ct-21 -->

- A. The eight 1 µm cubes have a total surface area of 48 µm² and a combined SA:V of 6:1, twice the 3:1 of the single cube.
- B. The single 2 µm cube has a total surface area of 48 µm² and an SA:V of 6:1, because it is one larger object.
- C. Both arrangements have an SA:V of 3:1, because their total volumes are the same.
- D. The eight 1 µm cubes have a total surface area of 24 µm², the same as the single cube.

- C. Cell Q has the greater SA:V, at 3.5:1 compared with 3:1 for cell P.
- D. Cell P has the greater SA:V, at 3:1 compared with about 2.9:1 for cell Q.

#### Prokaryotic and eukaryotic cells

**Q31.** Which structure is found in both prokaryotic and eukaryotic cells?
<!-- id: bio12a1-pe-1 -->

- A. Ribosomes
- B. Mitochondria
- C. Nuclear envelope
- D. Golgi apparatus

**Q32.** A newly discovered single-celled organism has a cell wall, circular DNA in the cytosol, plasmids and no membrane-bound organelles. It is best classified as
<!-- id: bio12a1-pe-2 -->

- A. a plant cell.
- B. a fungal cell.
- C. a protist.
- D. a prokaryote.

**Q33.** Which feature would distinguish a plant cell from an animal cell under a light microscope?
<!-- id: bio12a1-pe-3 -->

- A. Presence of a nucleus
- B. Presence of a cell wall and large central vacuole
- C. Presence of a plasma membrane
- D. Presence of cytoplasm

**Q34.** The typical size range of prokaryotic cells compared with eukaryotic cells is
<!-- id: bio12a1-pe-4 -->

- A. prokaryotes 10–100 µm; eukaryotes 1–10 µm.
- B. prokaryotes 0.1–5 µm; eukaryotes 10–100 µm.
- C. both 10–100 µm.
- D. prokaryotes 0.1–5 nm; eukaryotes 10–100 nm.

**Q35.** Which statement about the DNA of prokaryotes is correct?
<!-- id: bio12a1-pe-5 -->

- A. It is linear and wrapped around histones inside a nucleus.
- B. It is enclosed in a double membrane.
- C. It is found only in plasmids.
- D. It is a single circular chromosome located in the nucleoid region, often with plasmids.

<!-- id: bio12a1-pe-11 -->

- A. They carry the main chromosome that controls cell division.
- B. They carry small extra genes, such as antibiotic resistance, that can be passed between bacteria.
- C. They store glycogen as an energy reserve.
- D. They are the site of aerobic respiration.

<!-- id: bio12a1-pe-12 -->

- A. 2500 µm.
- B. 50 µm.
- C. 0.005 µm.
- D. 5 µm.

**Q43.** Why can a transmission electron microscope reveal structures such as ribosomes that a light microscope cannot resolve?
<!-- id: bio12a1-pe-13 -->

- A. Electrons have a much shorter wavelength than visible light, giving much higher resolution.
- B. Electron microscopes use coloured stains that light cannot pass through.
- C. Electron microscopes can view living cells in their natural state.
- D. Electron microscopes magnify only about ×2 but keep the specimen in full colour.

**Q44.** Which observation provides the strongest evidence for the endosymbiotic theory of the origin of mitochondria?
<!-- id: bio12a1-pe-14 -->

- A. Mitochondria have their own circular DNA and 70S ribosomes, similar to bacteria.
- B. Mitochondria are found only in animal cells.
- C. Mitochondria contain chlorophyll for photosynthesis.
- D. Mitochondria are surrounded by a single membrane, like lysosomes.

**Q45.** Which bacterial structure is a sticky outer layer that can help the bacterium resist attack by the host's immune cells?
<!-- id: bio12a1-pe-15 -->

- A. Flagellum.
- B. Ribosome.
- C. Nucleoid.
- D. Capsule.

**Q46.** Most prokaryotes reproduce by binary fission. Which statement best explains why bacterial populations can grow rapidly?
<!-- id: bio12a1-pe-16 -->

- A. Meiosis produces four genetically different cells, increasing variation.
- B. Mitosis divides chromosomes within a membrane-bound nucleus to make two identical cells.
- C. Binary fission produces two genetically identical cells, and under favourable conditions this can happen quickly.
- D. Cells fuse together to form one larger cell, so the number of cells shrinks.

**Q47.** Which of the following organisms is a eukaryote?
<!-- id: bio12a1-pe-17 -->

- A. Escherichia coli.
- B. Saccharomyces cerevisiae (baker's yeast).
- C. Streptococcus.
- D. Cyanobacteria (blue-green algae).

<!-- id: bio12a1-pe-18 -->

- A. 38.
- B. 80.
- C. 20.
- D. 2.

- C. Penicillin acts only on viruses, which are not cells.

#### Plasma membrane structure

<!-- id: bio12a1-pm-2 -->

- B. M
- C. G2
- D. S

- A. 0.4 hours
- B. 1 hour
- C. 2 hours
- D. 10 hours

<!-- id: bio12a1-cc-5 -->

- A. a cell plate forms between the two nuclei.

- D. the nucleus divides after the cytoplasm.

- B. It is a membrane-bound organelle that organises microtubules.
- C. It is the region where sister chromatids are joined and where spindle fibres attach.

<!-- id: bio12a1-cc-12 -->

- A. Chromosomes line up along the metaphase plate.
- B. Sister chromatids separate at their centromeres.
- C. Chromosomes condense and become visible under the microscope.
- D. Nuclear envelopes reform around each set of chromosomes at the poles.

**Q218.** What is the role of the centrosomes in an animal cell during mitosis?
<!-- id: bio12a1-cc-13 -->

- A. They organise the spindle microtubules that pull sister chromatids apart.
- B. They produce the ATP needed to replicate DNA.
- C. They attach sister chromatids to each other before replication.
- D. They form the cell plate that divides the cytoplasm.

**Q219.** What type of protein do spindle fibres consist of?
<!-- id: bio12a1-cc-14 -->

- A. Actin
- B. Tubulin
- C. Collagen
- D. Keratin

**Q220.** What is the function of cyclins in the cell cycle?
<!-- id: bio12a1-cc-15 -->

- A. They are structural proteins that form the spindle fibres.
- B. They are enzymes that cut DNA at the centromere.
- C. They bind cyclin-dependent kinases (CDKs) and regulate progression through the cell cycle.
- D. They attach sister chromatids to the nuclear envelope.

**Q221.** The mitotic index of a tissue sample is calculated as:
<!-- id: bio12a1-cc-16 -->

- A. the number of cells in mitosis divided by the total number of cells observed
- B. the total number of cells observed divided by the number in mitosis
- C. the number of cells in interphase divided by the number in mitosis
- D. the number of chromosomes divided by the number of cells observed

**Q222.** Which statement correctly distinguishes mitosis from cytokinesis?
<!-- id: bio12a1-cc-17 -->

- A. Mitosis divides the cytoplasm, while cytokinesis divides the nucleus.
- B. Mitosis divides the nucleus, while cytokinesis divides the cytoplasm to form two cells.
- C. Mitosis produces four haploid cells, while cytokinesis produces two diploid cells.
- D. Mitosis occurs in G1, while cytokinesis occurs during S phase.

**Q223.** Which statement best describes the time a typical dividing cell spends in interphase compared with mitosis?
<!-- id: bio12a1-cc-18 -->

- A. Mitosis occupies most of the cell cycle because cells divide continuously.
- B. Interphase and mitosis last the same length of time in most cells.
- C. Interphase lasts only a few minutes, as the cell spends most of its time dividing.
- D. Interphase occupies most of the cell cycle, while mitosis is comparatively brief.

<!-- id: bio12a1-cc-19 -->

- A. They are copies made by DNA replication of the same chromosome.
- B. They are the products of crossing over during prophase.
- C. They were inherited from two different parents.
- D. They are the same as the homologous chromosome of the pair.

- C. The cell proceeds to cytokinesis, producing a daughter cell with an extra chromosome.
- D. The chromosomes replicate again during G2.

<!-- id: bio12a1-pl-9 -->

<!-- id: bio12a1-pl-10 -->

<!-- id: bio12a1-pl-13 -->

<!-- id: bio12a1-pl-14 -->

- A. transpiration pulls sap up the sieve tubes from the roots.

- C. sieve plates act as pumps, moving sap in both directions.

### Unit 1 AOS 3: Homeostasis

#### Stimulus-response model and negative feedback

**Q388.** Which sequence correctly shows the stimulus-response model?
<!-- id: bio12a2-srm-1 -->

- A. Stimulus → modulator → receptor → effector → response
- B. Receptor → stimulus → effector → modulator → response
- C. Stimulus → receptor → modulator → effector → response
- D. Stimulus → effector → receptor → modulator → response

**Q389.** Negative feedback is best described as a mechanism in which
<!-- id: bio12a2-srm-2 -->

- A. the response increases the size of the original change.
- B. the response counteracts the original change, returning the variable towards its set point.
- C. the body stops responding to a stimulus after repeated exposure.
- D. receptors are switched off so the variable can change freely.

<!-- id: bio12a2-srm-3 -->

- A. modulator.
- B. receptor.
- C. effector.
- D. stimulus.

- D. Release of oxytocin during childbirth, which causes stronger contractions that trigger more oxytocin release

<!-- id: bio12a2-srm-5 -->

<!-- id: bio12a2-srm-11 -->

**Q399.** After a meal, blood glucose rises and insulin is released, causing body cells to take up glucose and the liver to store it. Which statement about this pathway is correct?
<!-- id: bio12a2-srm-12 -->

- A. It is positive feedback, because insulin raises blood glucose further.
- B. It is negative feedback, because insulin increases the original rise in blood glucose.
- C. It is negative feedback, because the response lowers blood glucose back towards the set point.
- D. It is not feedback, because the response does not involve the nervous system.

**Q400.** Which statement about blood clotting is correct?
<!-- id: bio12a2-srm-13 -->

- A. It is negative feedback, because the clot reverses the cut in the blood vessel.
- B. It is positive feedback, because each clotting factor activates more clotting factors, amplifying the response until a clot forms.
- C. It is negative feedback, because platelets stop clotting once bleeding starts.
- D. It is not a feedback process, because it does not involve a receptor.

**Q401.** Normal arterial blood pH is 7.35–7.45. Which value lies outside this normal range?
<!-- id: bio12a2-srm-14 -->

- A. 7.38
- B. 7.42
- C. 7.31
- D. 7.36

**Q402.** When the blood becomes more concentrated, osmoreceptors in the hypothalamus detect the change and more ADH is released. What is the effect of ADH on the kidney, and on the blood?
<!-- id: bio12a2-srm-15 -->

- A. The collecting ducts reabsorb more water, so the blood becomes more dilute.
- B. The collecting ducts reabsorb less water, so the blood becomes more dilute.
- C. The collecting ducts reabsorb more water, so the blood becomes more concentrated.
- D. The collecting ducts reabsorb more salt, so the blood becomes more concentrated.

**Q403.** Why do most homeostatic control systems use negative feedback rather than positive feedback?
<!-- id: bio12a2-srm-16 -->

- A. Negative feedback makes the variable change faster, which is needed in emergencies.
- B. Negative feedback keeps the variable close to its set point, so conditions stay within the limits cells need.
- C. Negative feedback removes the need for receptors and effectors.
- D. Negative feedback allows the stimulus to be maintained indefinitely.

**Q404.** A person has not eaten for several hours and their blood glucose begins to fall. In the stimulus-response model, the stimulus is the
<!-- id: bio12a2-srm-17 -->

- A. release of glucagon from the pancreas.
- B. release of glucose from the liver.
- C. uptake of glucose by body cells.
- D. fall in blood glucose concentration.

**Q405.** Which of the following is a response rather than a stimulus?
<!-- id: bio12a2-srm-18 -->

- A. Shivering, the rapid contraction of skeletal muscles that generates heat.
- B. A fall in environmental temperature.
- C. A rise in blood pressure detected by stretch receptors.
- D. An increase in blood carbon dioxide concentration.

**Q406.** A homeostatic response reduces a deviation from the set point, but the variable overshoots the set point before settling back. How should this pattern be classified?
<!-- id: bio12a2-srm-19 -->

- A. Positive feedback, because the variable moved past the set point.
- B. Negative feedback, because the response still acts to reduce the deviation from the set point.
- C. Positive feedback, because the response needed more than one correction.
- D. Not feedback, because the variable did not stay at the set point.

**Q407.** What is the main purpose of a positive feedback loop in the body?
<!-- id: bio12a2-srm-20 -->

- A. To keep a variable within a narrow range around its set point.
- B. To switch off the stimulus once it has been detected.
- C. To rapidly drive a process to completion, such as childbirth or blood clotting.
- D. To stop receptors from responding to repeated stimuli.

**Q408.** A person is exposed to cold air and their core body temperature begins to fall. Which part of the pathway is the effector?
<!-- id: bio12a2-srm-21 -->

- A. Thermoreceptors in the skin.
- B. The hypothalamus, which compares the temperature with the set point.
- C. The fall in core body temperature.
- D. The skeletal muscles, which contract to shiver and produce heat.

#### Thermoregulation

<!-- id: bio12a2-thermo-1 -->

<!-- id: bio12a2-thermo-3 -->

<!-- id: bio12a2-thermo-4 -->

<!-- id: bio12a2-thermo-5 -->

- B. structural thermoregulation by an ectotherm.
- C. behavioural thermoregulation by an ectotherm.

**Q427.** A runner's core temperature rises above the set point during a race. Which sequence correctly describes how the body responds?
<!-- id: bio12a2-thermo-10 -->

- A. Sweat glands secrete sweat, the hypothalamus detects the rise, thermoreceptors respond, and evaporation cools the skin.
- B. Thermoreceptors detect the rise, the hypothalamus receives the impulses, sweat glands secrete sweat, and evaporation removes heat from the skin.
- C. The hypothalamus secretes sweat, thermoreceptors send impulses to the sweat glands, and evaporation removes heat from the skin.
- D. The thyroid gland releases thyroxine, the hypothalamus sends impulses to skeletal muscles, and shivering increases heat production.

**Q428.** The large, thin ears of the desert fennec fox (Vulpes zerda) help the animal survive hot conditions mainly because they
<!-- id: bio12a2-thermo-11 -->

- A. trap a layer of warm air against the head, reducing heat gain from the Sun.
- B. absorb solar radiation, which warms the blood flowing through them.
- C. have a large surface area with many blood vessels close to the skin, so heat is lost by radiation and convection.
- D. reduce heat loss by stopping blood flow to the ears.

**Q429.** Huddling together in a tight group during a blizzard is an example of which type of response to a cold stimulus?
<!-- id: bio12a2-thermo-12 -->

- A. A physiological response that is controlled by the hypothalamus.
- B. A physiological response that increases heat production.
- C. An endocrine response mediated by thyroxine.
- D. A behavioural response that reduces heat loss.

**Q430.** Which statement about ectotherms is correct?
<!-- id: bio12a2-thermo-13 -->

- A. They rely mainly on heat from the external environment and on behaviour to regulate their body temperature.
- B. They maintain a constant core temperature by metabolic heat production alone.
- C. Their body temperature is always higher than that of endotherms of the same size.
- D. They cannot change their behaviour to alter how much heat they gain.

**Q431.** Why does a mouse lose heat from its body faster, per gram of body mass, than an elephant in the same cold environment?
<!-- id: bio12a2-thermo-14 -->

- A. A mouse has a lower metabolic rate per gram of body mass, so it produces less heat.
- B. A mouse has a larger surface area to volume ratio, so more heat is lost through its surface for each gram of body tissue.
- C. An elephant has more sweat glands per gram of body mass, so it loses more heat by evaporation.
- D. A mouse has a lower core temperature than an elephant, so heat flows out faster.

**Q432.** Why can a person in water at 20 °C lose body heat faster than a person standing in air at 20 °C?
<!-- id: bio12a2-thermo-15 -->

- A. Water is denser than air, so it pushes heat out of the skin by pressure.
- B. Water removes heat from the skin by evaporation more quickly than air does.
- C. Water conducts heat away from the skin much more rapidly than air does.
- D. Water stops the hypothalamus from detecting the change in body temperature.

**Q433.** During an infection, pyrogens act on the hypothalamus and raise the set point to 39 °C. Which response occurs as the core temperature rises towards the new set point?
<!-- id: bio12a2-thermo-16 -->

- A. Sweat glands increase their secretion to cool the blood.
- B. Arterioles supplying the skin dilate, increasing heat loss by radiation.
- C. Thermoreceptors stop sending impulses to the hypothalamus.
- D. Vasoconstriction and shivering increase heat production and reduce heat loss.

**Q434.** A hiker loses a large volume of water through sweating and does not drink enough. Why does dehydration reduce the body's ability to cool itself?
<!-- id: bio12a2-thermo-17 -->

- A. The hypothalamus can no longer detect a rise in core temperature.
- B. Less water is available to be secreted as sweat, so less sweat evaporates and less heat is removed.
- C. Sweat becomes hotter than the skin, so it cannot evaporate.
- D. Vasodilation of the arterioles is blocked, so blood cannot flow near the skin.

**Q435.** Which statement correctly describes negative feedback in the control of body temperature?
<!-- id: bio12a2-thermo-18 -->

- A. The response to a change in temperature increases that change, moving the temperature further from the set point.
- B. The response occurs only after the stimulus has stopped.
- C. The responses counteract the change, moving the core temperature back towards the set point.
- D. The responses have no effect on the stimulus that started them.

<!-- id: bio12a2-thermo-19 -->

- C. Sweat contains large amounts of urea, which raises blood osmolarity.
- D. Sweating increases the glomerular filtration rate, so less water is reabsorbed.

#### Malfunctions of homeostasis

**Q504.** Type 1 diabetes mellitus is caused by
<!-- id: bio12a2-mal-1 -->

- A. the immune system destroying the beta cells of the pancreas, so little or no insulin is produced.
- B. body cells becoming less responsive to insulin.
- C. the posterior pituitary failing to release ADH.
- D. the liver producing too much glucagon.

**Q505.** Which statement best describes type 2 diabetes mellitus in its early stages?
<!-- id: bio12a2-mal-2 -->

- A. The pancreas cannot produce any insulin.
- B. It is always caused by a single inherited allele.
- C. Insulin is produced, but target cells respond poorly to it (insulin resistance).
- D. Blood glucose is abnormally low.

**Q506.** Two adults fasted overnight and then had their blood glucose and blood insulin measured. Person P: glucose 9.5 mmol L⁻¹, insulin higher than normal. Person Q: glucose 12 mmol L⁻¹, insulin almost undetectable. The most likely conclusion is that
<!-- id: bio12a2-mal-3 -->

- A. P has type 1 diabetes and Q has type 2 diabetes.
- B. both P and Q have type 1 diabetes.
- C. P is healthy and Q has diabetes insipidus.
- D. P has type 2 diabetes and Q has type 1 diabetes.

**Q507.** In Graves' disease, the thyroid gland secretes too much thyroxine. Which symptom would be expected?
<!-- id: bio12a2-mal-4 -->

- A. Weight gain and feeling cold all the time
- B. Weight loss, a fast heart rate and feeling hot
- C. Very low blood glucose after meals
- D. Producing large volumes of dilute urine because ADH is absent

**Q508.** Glucose is often found in the urine of a person with untreated diabetes mellitus because
<!-- id: bio12a2-mal-5 -->

- A. the glomerulus becomes permeable to glucose only in diabetes.
- B. insulin is normally needed for glucose to be filtered.
- C. the amount of glucose in the filtrate is greater than the nephron's carriers can reabsorb.
- D. glucose is actively secreted into the collecting duct.

**Q512.** A person with type 1 diabetes injects too much insulin. Which homeostatic response would help restore blood glucose?
<!-- id: bio12a2-mal-9 -->

- A. Beta cells release more insulin, which lowers blood glucose further.
- B. Alpha cells release glucagon, which stimulates the liver to break down glycogen and release glucose.
- C. The posterior pituitary releases more ADH so that glucose is excreted in the urine.
- D. The thyroid releases less thyroxine, which raises blood glucose immediately.

**Q513.** Regular aerobic exercise helps people with type 2 diabetes lower their blood glucose. Which explanation is most accurate?
<!-- id: bio12a2-mal-10 -->

- A. Exercise stimulates the pancreas to secrete more glucagon, which lowers blood glucose.
- B. Exercise causes the liver to convert glucose into fat, permanently removing insulin resistance.
- C. Working muscles take up glucose from the blood, even when the response to insulin is weak.
- D. Exercise increases the release of ADH, which removes glucose from the blood in the urine.

**Q514.** Why is iodine essential for the production of thyroxine?
<!-- id: bio12a2-mal-11 -->

- A. Iodine is a component of the thyroxine molecule, so thyroxine cannot be made without it.
- B. Iodine activates the liver enzyme that breaks down glycogen.
- C. Iodine is needed to make insulin, which thyroid cells use to divide.
- D. Iodine is required for ADH to bind to receptors in the collecting ducts.

**Q515.** A person passes large volumes of dilute urine. Which test result would point to diabetes insipidus rather than diabetes mellitus?
<!-- id: bio12a2-mal-12 -->

- A. Urine contains glucose, and blood glucose is high.
- B. Urine is concentrated and contains glucose.
- C. Blood glucose is high, and urine contains ketones.
- D. Urine contains no glucose, and blood glucose is normal.

**Q516.** Why does untreated type 1 diabetes sometimes lead to ketoacidosis?
<!-- id: bio12a2-mal-13 -->

- A. Excess insulin causes the liver to release large amounts of ketones into the blood.
- B. Without insulin, cells cannot use glucose effectively, so fats are broken down for energy and acidic ketones build up.
- C. The kidneys reabsorb too much glucose, which makes the blood acidic.
- D. ADH secretion increases, concentrating ketones in the blood.

**Q517.** During a fever caused by an infection, what happens in the hypothalamus?
<!-- id: bio12a2-mal-14 -->

- A. It stops detecting body temperature, so sweating stops.
- B. It increases heat loss by dilating skin arterioles to cool the body.
- C. Its temperature set point is raised, so the body conserves and produces heat until it reaches the new set point.
- D. It secretes TSH, which increases metabolic rate in all cells until the fever breaks.

**Q518.** During haemodialysis, how does urea move across the dialysis membrane?

- B. From the dialysis fluid into the blood, because the fluid contains more urea.
- C. Only by active transport using ATP, so that it is removed from the blood faster.
- D. Not at all, because the membrane is impermeable to all waste products.

**Q520.** Why does diabetes mellitus with very high blood glucose cause increased urine volume (polyuria)?

- C. High blood glucose stimulates ADH secretion, which increases water loss.

### Unit 2 AOS 1: Inheritance

#### Genomes, genes and alleles

**Q531.** An organism's genome is best defined as
<!-- id: bio12a2-gen-1 -->

- A. the set of alleles it has for one trait.
- B. the complete set of genetic material (DNA) present in its cells.
- C. all the proteins expressed by one cell.
- D. the genes found on its sex chromosomes.

<!-- id: bio12a2-gen-2 -->

- A. a section of DNA that never codes for a protein.

- C. one of the alternative forms of a gene, found at the same locus.
- D. a pair of identical chromosomes.

- B. It contains about 3 million base pairs, all of which code for proteins.
- C. It contains about 200 000 protein-coding genes.

<!-- id: bio12a2-gen-4 -->

- B. haploid for the wing shape gene.
- C. homozygous dominant for wing shape.

<!-- id: bio12a2-gen-5 -->

- C. the type of sugar in the DNA backbone.

<!-- id: bio12a2-gen-14 -->

- C. Each individual has three copies of the gene in every body cell.

#### Meiosis and variation

**Q591.** Crossing over occurs during
<!-- id: bio12a2-mei-1 -->

- A. prophase I, between non-sister chromatids of homologous chromosomes.
- B. metaphase II, between sister chromatids.
- C. anaphase I, when homologous chromosomes are pulled apart.

**Q592.** Independent assortment during meiosis is the result of
<!-- id: bio12a2-mei-2 -->

- A. DNA replication during interphase.
- B. crossing over at chiasmata.
- C. separation of sister chromatids in anaphase II.
- D. the random orientation of homologous pairs at the cell equator during metaphase I.

**Q593.** A species of plant has a diploid number of 8 (2n = 8). Ignoring crossing over, how many genetically different combinations of chromosomes can its gametes have due to independent assortment?
<!-- id: bio12a2-mei-3 -->

- B. 8
- C. 16
- D. 64

<!-- id: bio12a2-mei-4 -->

- A. two genetically identical diploid cells.
- B. four genetically different haploid cells.
- C. four genetically identical haploid cells.
- D. two genetically different diploid cells.

<!-- id: bio12a2-mei-5 -->

- A. all normal.
- B. half normal and half abnormal.
- C. all abnormal: two with an extra chromosome and two missing a chromosome.
- D. all triploid.

**Q599.** During prophase I of meiosis, homologous chromosomes pair up. What is this pair of homologous chromosomes, with four chromatids in total, called?
<!-- id: bio12a2-mei-9 -->

- A. A bivalent, also called a tetrad.
- B. A dyad, made of two sister chromatids joined at the centromere.
- C. A single chromosome with two centromeres.
- D. A pair of sister chromatids that separate at anaphase I.

**Q600.** A horse has 64 chromosomes in each body cell. How many chromosomes does each egg cell contain?
<!-- id: bio12a2-mei-10 -->

- A. 64
- B. 32
- C. 128
- D. 16

**Q601.** Which process is the original source of new alleles in a population?
<!-- id: bio12a2-mei-11 -->

- A. Crossing over, which exchanges segments between non-sister chromatids.
- B. Independent assortment, which sorts homologous pairs randomly at metaphase I.
- C. Mutation, which changes the base sequence of DNA.
- D. Random fertilisation, which combines gametes from two parents.

**Q602.** During which stage of meiosis do sister chromatids separate and move to opposite poles?

- D. Anaphase II.

**Q603.** Which statement best describes a pair of homologous chromosomes?
<!-- id: bio12a2-mei-13 -->

- A. One chromosome from each parent, carrying genes for the same characteristics at the same loci.
- B. Two identical copies of one chromosome, joined at the centromere after DNA replication.
- C. The X and Y chromosomes, which determine sex in humans.
- D. Two chromosomes of the same size, always found in the same parent.

**Q604.** Sister chromatids are first formed when
<!-- id: bio12a2-mei-14 -->

- A. homologous chromosomes separate at anaphase I.
- B. DNA replicates during interphase, before meiosis begins.

- D. two gametes fuse at fertilisation.

**Q605.** In humans, in which tissues does meiosis take place?

- A. Skin and liver.
- B. Bone marrow.
- C. The lining of the intestine.

**Q606.** An individual has genotype AaBb, with the two genes on different pairs of homologous chromosomes. What percentage of its gametes are expected to be AB?
<!-- id: bio12a2-mei-16 -->

- A. 25%

- C. 50%

- C. 6 bivalents, 12 chromatids

**Q608.** A karyotype shows 47 chromosomes, with three copies of chromosome 21 and two copies of every other chromosome. This is an example of
<!-- id: bio12a2-mei-18 -->

- B. polyploidy, caused by an extra complete set of chromosomes.
- C. trisomy 21, usually caused by nondisjunction during meiosis.

**Q609.** At the end of meiosis I, each daughter cell is
<!-- id: bio12a2-mei-19 -->

#### Genotype, phenotype and environment

**Q621.** An organism's phenotype is best described as
<!-- id: bio12a2-gpe-1 -->

- A. the alleles it carries for a particular gene.
- B. its observable characteristics, resulting from the interaction of its genotype with the environment.
- C. the complete set of its DNA.

**Q622.** Two cuttings taken from the same hydrangea plant were planted in different gardens. One produced blue flowers in acidic soil and the other produced pink flowers in alkaline soil. The best explanation is that
<!-- id: bio12a2-gpe-2 -->

- B. a mutation occurred in one cutting.
- C. flower colour in hydrangeas is controlled by the environment only.
- D. the same genotype produced different phenotypes because of differences in the environment.

<!-- id: bio12a2-gpe-3 -->

- A. dark, because the enzyme is active at the lower temperature.
- B. pale, because the cat's genotype for the body is different.
- C. pale, because shaving removes the pigment genes.

**Q633.** A trait in a population shows continuous variation. Which statement is most likely to be correct?

- A. The trait is usually controlled by many genes and influenced by the environment.
- B. The trait is controlled by a single gene with two alleles.
- C. Individuals fall into a small number of distinct classes.
- D. The trait cannot be measured on a numerical scale.

**Q634.** Snowshoe hares grow brown coats in summer and white coats in winter. In an experiment, hares kept at a winter temperature but exposed to a summer day length kept their brown coats. Which conclusion is best supported?
<!-- id: bio12a2-gpe-14 -->

- A. Temperature is the only environmental factor that can change coat colour.
- B. Coat colour is fixed by genotype and cannot change with the environment.
- C. The hares must have mutated to grow brown coats in summer.
- D. Day length is an environmental factor that changes coat colour, so one genotype can produce different phenotypes at different times of year.

**Q635.** A dairy cow genotype has a narrow norm of reaction for milk yield, with yields of 6 000 L to 6 500 L across all farms. Which interpretation is best?
<!-- id: bio12a2-gpe-15 -->

- A. The trait is controlled by many genes with large effects, so it shows discrete categories.
- B. The environment has no effect on milk yield at all.
- C. The genotype gives a similar phenotype across these farms, so the environment has only a small effect on milk yield.
- D. The genotype has mutated on each farm.

<!-- id: bio12a2-gpe-16 -->

- C. Seedlings of different species, with and without fertiliser
- D. One seedling given fertiliser, with its height compared with results from a previous year

**Q637.** Why does a trait controlled by several genes usually show a wider range of phenotypes than a trait controlled by one gene?

**Q638.** Which statement about the link between genes and phenotype is most accurate?

#### Dihybrid crosses and linked genes

**Q680.** In peas, yellow seed (Y) is dominant to green (y) and round seed (R) is dominant to wrinkled (r). The genes are on different chromosomes. What is the expected phenotypic ratio from the cross YyRr × YyRr?
<!-- id: bio12a3-dihy-1 -->

- A. 9 : 3 : 3 : 1
- B. 1 : 1 : 1 : 1
- C. 3 : 1
- D. 1 : 2 : 1

**Q681.** Which gametes can be produced by an individual with genotype AaBb, assuming the two genes are unlinked?
<!-- id: bio12a3-dihy-2 -->

- A. AB and ab only
- B. Aa and Bb
- C. AB, Ab, aB and ab in equal proportions
- D. AA, BB, aa and bb

**Q682.** A fruit fly heterozygous for two genes (genotype GgLl, with G and L inherited from the same parent) is test crossed with a ggll fly. The offspring are: 420 GgLl, 410 ggll, 85 Ggll and 85 ggLl. What best explains these results?
<!-- id: bio12a3-dihy-3 -->

- A. The genes are unlinked and are assorting independently
- B. The genes are linked, and the smaller classes are recombinants produced by crossing over
- C. The genes are linked and no crossing over occurred

- A. 8.3%
- B. 17%
- C. 20.5%
- D. 83%

<!-- id: bio12a3-dihy-5 -->

- A. never be separated during meiosis

- C. show a higher recombination frequency, approaching that of unlinked genes
- D. always be inherited together in the same gamete

**Q685.** In guinea pigs, black coat (B) is dominant to white (b) and rough coat (R) is dominant to smooth (r). The genes are unlinked. A black, rough guinea pig of genotype BbRr is crossed with a white, smooth guinea pig (bbrr). (a) List the gametes produced by each parent. (b) State the expected phenotypes of the offspring and their ratio. (3 marks)
<!-- id: bio12a3-dihy-6 -->

#### Pedigree charts and modes of inheritance

### Unit 2 AOS 2: Reproduction and adaptation

#### Asexual reproduction

**Q740.** Which of the following is an example of vegetative propagation in plants?
<!-- id: bio12a3-asex-1 -->

- A. A wattle producing seeds after pollination by birds
- B. Strawberry plants producing new plants from runners
- C. A fern releasing spores formed by meiosis
- D. Pollen tubes growing down the style of a flower

**Q741.** Yeast cells commonly reproduce by forming a small outgrowth that pinches off from the parent cell. This form of asexual reproduction is called
<!-- id: bio12a3-asex-2 -->

- A. binary fission
- B. budding
- C. fragmentation
- D. sporogenesis

**Q742.** Some female Komodo dragons in zoos have produced offspring without mating, by parthenogenesis. Which statement about parthenogenesis is correct?
<!-- id: bio12a3-asex-3 -->

- A. It involves fertilisation of an egg by sperm stored from a previous mating
- B. An unfertilised egg develops into a new individual
- C. It only occurs in bacteria and archaea
- D. It always produces offspring identical to the father

**Q743.** A farmer grows a field of genetically identical banana plants propagated from suckers. Which is the greatest risk of this practice?
<!-- id: bio12a3-asex-4 -->

- A. The plants will take longer to reach maturity than seedlings
- B. A single new disease could infect and kill the whole crop because there is little genetic variation
- C. The plants will produce offspring with many new mutations
- D. The plants will be unable to photosynthesise efficiently

**Q744.** Which statement correctly compares asexual reproduction with sexual reproduction?
<!-- id: bio12a3-asex-5 -->

- A. Asexual reproduction needs two parents and produces variable offspring
- B. Asexual reproduction allows rapid population growth in a stable environment because every individual can produce offspring
- C. Asexual reproduction involves meiosis and fertilisation
- D. Asexual reproduction produces greater genetic diversity than sexual reproduction

*Written answer*

- A. Meiosis of gametes released from the wound
- B. Binary fission of the differentiated cells at the cut surface
- C. Mitosis of stem cells (neoblasts) that replace the missing tissues
- D. Fertilisation between cells from different pieces

<!-- id: bio12a3-asex-10 -->

- A. A taproot
- B. A rhizome
- C. A seed
- D. A fruit

**Q750.** Bread mould (Rhizopus) produces spores inside sporangia at the tips of its aerial hyphae. These spores are produced asexually. Which statement about them is correct?
<!-- id: bio12a3-asex-11 -->

- A. They are formed by meiosis, so each spore is genetically different from the parent
- B. They are formed by binary fission, so each spore has half the parent's genetic material
- C. They are formed by fertilisation of two hyphae, so they are genetically varied
- D. They are formed by mitosis, so they are genetically identical to the parent

**Q751.** Which of the following is NOT an example of asexual reproduction?
<!-- id: bio12a3-asex-12 -->

- A. Sunflower seed formation after cross-pollination by bees
- B. Tuber formation in a potato plant
- C. Binary fission in a bacterium
- D. Root suckers forming from the roots of a poplar tree

**Q752.** Which crop is most commonly propagated by planting sections of stem, rather than from seed?
<!-- id: bio12a3-asex-13 -->

- A. Wheat
- B. Maize
- C. Sugarcane
- D. Rice

**Q753.** A bacterium divides by binary fission every 20 minutes. Starting with one cell, how many cells are present after 2 hours, assuming no cells die?
<!-- id: bio12a3-asex-14 -->

- A. 12
- B. 64
- C. 32
- D. 6

**Q754.** A somatic (body cell) mutation occurs in one bud of a tree. The branch grown from that bud carries the mutation, but the rest of the tree does not. Which best explains this?
<!-- id: bio12a3-asex-15 -->

- A. The mutation was in a cell whose mitotic descendants form only that branch, so the other parts of the tree did not inherit it
- B. Meiosis in the bud produced gametes carrying the mutation, which then spread to other branches
- C. The mutation was caused by pollen from another tree fertilising the bud
- D. The branch has a different number of chromosomes from the rest of the tree

**Q755.** In honeybees, drones (males) develop from unfertilised eggs, while workers (females) develop from fertilised eggs. Which statement about drones is correct?
<!-- id: bio12a3-asex-16 -->

- A. They are diploid, with the same chromosome number as the queen
- B. They have a father, because they develop from a fertilised egg as workers do
- C. They have two fathers, because they inherit genes from two separate eggs
- D. They are haploid, with half the chromosome number of the females

**Q756.** In somatic cell nuclear transfer, a cell from an adult animal's body is used to create a clone. Before the donor nucleus is inserted into an egg cell, what is removed from the egg?

- A. Its mitochondria
- B. Its cytoplasm
- C. Its own nucleus
- D. Its cell membrane

**Q757.** A grafted apple tree is made by joining a shoot (scion) of one apple cultivar onto the root system (rootstock) of another. Which statement is correct?
<!-- id: bio12a3-asex-18 -->

- A. The scion grows from cells produced by mitosis, so it is genetically identical to the donor cultivar, while the rootstock keeps its own genotype
- B. The grafted tree is a hybrid, because the scion and rootstock fuse their genetic material through fertilisation
- C. Seeds from the grafted tree's apples will be genetically identical to the scion cultivar
- D. Grafting is a form of sexual reproduction, because the two plants combine their gametes

**Q758.** A hydra reproduces asexually by budding. A bud forms as a small outgrowth on the body wall. What is the genetic makeup of the bud compared with the parent hydra?

- B. It is genetically identical, because its cells are produced by mitosis from the parent's cells
- C. It has half the genes of the parent and half of a second hydra
- D. It is identical only in its tentacles, because the rest is formed by fertilisation

#### Sexual reproduction and genetic diversity

- C. They are genetically identical to the parent, apart from any new mutations

**Q782.** Two parents each have 2n = 6. Ignoring crossing over, how many different zygotes can be formed when their gametes fuse at random?
<!-- id: bio12a3-sex-12 -->

- A. 8
- B. 16
- C. 32
- D. 64

**Q783.** Which statement correctly compares oogenesis and spermatogenesis?
<!-- id: bio12a3-sex-13 -->

- A. One primary oocyte forms one functional ovum and polar bodies, while one primary spermatocyte forms four functional sperm
- B. Both processes produce four equal-sized functional gametes from one precursor cell
- C. Oogenesis produces four functional ova, while spermatogenesis produces only one sperm
- D. Polar bodies are a normal product of spermatogenesis, making each sperm smaller

**Q784.** Which process is the ultimate source of new alleles in a population?
<!-- id: bio12a3-sex-14 -->

- A. Crossing over
- B. Mutation
- C. Independent assortment
- D. Random fertilisation

**Q785.** Cavendish bananas are propagated from suckers, so almost all plants in a plantation are genetically identical. What is the main risk to the crop?
<!-- id: bio12a3-sex-15 -->

- A. A pathogen that infects one plant can spread to nearly all of them, destroying much of the crop
- B. Crossing over will produce new alleles each season, making the crop unpredictable
- C. Each plant will produce gametes with half the normal chromosome number, reducing fruit yield
- D. Random fertilisation will produce zygotes with too many chromosomes

**Q786.** A gamete with 12 chromosomes fuses with another gamete with 12 chromosomes. What are the chromosome number and ploidy of the zygote?

- B. 6, haploid
- C. 24, haploid
- D. 24, diploid

**Q787.** What is a pair of sister chromatids?

- C. The two cells produced at the end of meiosis I
- D. The two sex chromosomes in a human male

**Q788.** What is genetic diversity within a population?

- A. The total number of individuals in the population

- C. The number of different species living in the same habitat
- D. The number of chromosomes in each cell of the population members

**Q789.** A human male has 47 chromosomes with an XXY karyotype (Klinefelter syndrome). What is the most likely cause?

- C. Non-disjunction of the sex chromosomes during meiosis in one parent, producing a gamete with two sex chromosomes
- D. Failure of DNA replication in the egg before fertilisation

**Q790.** A cell from an organism with 2n = 4 undergoes meiosis. How many chromosomes does each resulting cell contain?
<!-- id: bio12a3-sex-20 -->

- A. 1
- B. 2
- C. 4
- D. 8

#### Reproductive cloning

**Q800.** In somatic cell nuclear transfer (SCNT), the nucleus from a somatic cell of the animal to be cloned is inserted into
<!-- id: bio12a3-clone-1 -->

- A. a sperm cell from a male of the same species
- B. an egg cell whose nucleus has been removed (enucleated)
- C. a fertilised zygote that still contains its own nucleus
- D. a stem cell taken from bone marrow

**Q801.** Dolly the sheep was produced by SCNT using a nucleus from a Finn Dorset ewe's udder cell, an egg from a Scottish Blackface ewe, and a Scottish Blackface surrogate mother. Dolly's nuclear DNA was identical to that of
<!-- id: bio12a3-clone-2 -->

- A. the egg donor
- B. the surrogate mother
- C. the Finn Dorset nucleus donor
- D. a combination of all three sheep

**Q802.** Embryo splitting involves separating cells from an early embryo to produce several embryos that are implanted into surrogates. The offspring produced are
<!-- id: bio12a3-clone-3 -->

- A. genetically identical to the surrogate mothers
- B. genetically identical to each other but not identical to either parent
- C. genetically identical to the father only
- D. all genetically different from each other

**Q803.** Plant tissue culture (micropropagation) is used to produce large numbers of a rare orchid. Which statement is correct?
<!-- id: bio12a3-clone-4 -->

- A. The plantlets will show high genetic diversity because they form from many different cells
- B. Small pieces of tissue are grown on sterile nutrient media with plant hormones to form genetically identical plantlets
- C. The technique requires pollination and seed formation
- D. The plantlets are haploid because they come from meristem cells

**Q804.** Which is the most significant biological concern about using reproductive cloning to conserve an endangered species?
<!-- id: bio12a3-clone-5 -->

- A. Cloned animals cannot reproduce sexually
- B. Cloning adds no new genetic diversity to a population that already has little
- C. Cloned animals always have shorter lifespans than the donor
- D. Cloning changes the species' chromosome number

- C. the cytoplasm of the egg donor, which is not removed when the egg nucleus is removed

**Q809.** After a donor nucleus is placed in an enucleated egg, the reconstructed cell is given an electric pulse or chemicals. What is the purpose of this step?
<!-- id: bio12a3-clone-10 -->

- A. to activate the reconstructed egg so that it starts dividing as an embryo
- B. to remove the donor nucleus from the egg
- C. to cause meiosis so that the chromosome number is halved
- D. to fuse the egg with a sperm cell so that it is fertilised

**Q810.** In SCNT, the donor nucleus must be reprogrammed. What does this mean?
<!-- id: bio12a3-clone-11 -->

- A. the donor DNA is permanently mutated to match the egg's DNA
- B. the donor nucleus is replaced by a sperm nucleus
- C. the donor cell's chromosomes are halved by meiosis before transfer

**Q811.** Which statement correctly compares embryo splitting with somatic cell nuclear transfer (SCNT)?
<!-- id: bio12a3-clone-12 -->

- A. both produce offspring that are genetically identical to the parent that donated the nucleus
- B. embryo splitting produces offspring that are identical to each other, whereas SCNT produces offspring with the nuclear DNA of the body cell donor
- C. embryo splitting requires an enucleated egg, whereas SCNT does not
- D. both produce offspring that are genetically identical to the surrogate mother

**Q812.** Which plant tissue is most often used as the explant in micropropagation, and why?
<!-- id: bio12a3-clone-13 -->

- A. meristem tissue, because its cells divide actively and are often free of viruses
- B. mature xylem, because it is fully differentiated and stable
- C. pollen grains, because they are haploid and easy to sterilise

- A. root formation

<!-- id: bio12a3-clone-15 -->

- A. the donor nucleus is too large to fit inside the egg cell
- B. the surrogate's immune system destroys every embryo on contact
- C. all the donor cell's chromosomes carry faulty alleles from the egg

**Q815.** A bull is cloned by SCNT, using a body cell from the bull and an enucleated egg from a different cow. Which statement about the sex of the clone is correct?

- C. the clone's sex matches the nucleus donor, because the sex chromosomes are in the nucleus
- D. the clone's sex matches the egg donor, because the egg determines sex

#### Structural, physiological and behavioural adaptations

**Q829.** Which of the following is a behavioural adaptation?
<!-- id: bio12a3-adapt-1 -->

- A. The thick fur of a mountain pygmy possum
- B. A bearded dragon basking in the sun in the morning to raise its body temperature
- C. Concentrated urine produced by a hopping mouse
- D. The sunken stomata of a Banksia leaf

**Q830.** A desert mammal has a small surface area to volume ratio relative to a similar species living in a cooler habitat. Which statement about this feature is correct?

- B. It is a structural adaptation that reduces heat loss
- C. It is a structural adaptation that reduces heat gain from the environment and water loss
- D. It is a physiological adaptation that increases evaporative cooling

- C. Extensive deep root system - reaches groundwater
- D. Sunken stomata - traps humid air

<!-- id: bio12a3-adapt-4 -->

- A. hibernation
- B. aestivation

- B. 2 only
- C. 1 and 3
- D. 2 and 3

**Q838.** A bird living in a cold alpine environment has feathers that trap a layer of warm air close to its skin. This feature is best classified as
<!-- id: bio12a3-adapt-10 -->

- A. structural
- B. physiological
- C. behavioural
- D. biochemical

**Q839.** A meerkat group posts a sentry that gives a warning call when a hawk approaches, while the rest of the group keeps foraging. Which statement about the sentry is correct?

- B. It is a physiological adaptation that increases the sentry's alertness
- C. It is a behavioural adaptation that reduces the risk of predation for the group

**Q840.** Two cube-shaped models of an animal have sides of 2 cm and 4 cm. Which statement about surface area to volume ratio is correct?

- B. Both cubes have the same ratio, because both have the same shape
- C. The 2 cm cube has a ratio of 1.5 cm⁻¹ and the 4 cm cube has a ratio of 3 cm⁻¹

**Q841.** Which statement correctly describes the function of the thick, corky bark of a eucalypt trunk?

- A. It stores water that the tree can draw on during drought
- B. It insulates the living cambium beneath, protecting it from heat during bushfires
- C. It absorbs carbon dioxide for photosynthesis
- D. It produces hormones that control the timing of flowering

**Q842.** Desert mammals lose a large amount of water in exhaled breath. Which structural feature helps reduce this loss?
<!-- id: bio12a3-adapt-14 -->

- A. Convoluted nasal passages that cool exhaled air, so water vapour condenses and is returned to the body
- B. Rapid, shallow panting that increases evaporation from the tongue
- C. Large lungs with a high breathing rate to take in more oxygen
- D. Sweat glands spread over the whole body surface

**Q843.** Mangroves growing in waterlogged, low-oxygen mud produce aerial roots called pneumatophores. What is their main function?
<!-- id: bio12a3-adapt-15 -->

- A. They anchor the tree in soft mud and stop it being uprooted by tides
- B. They excrete excess salt from the plant into the surrounding mud
- C. They project above the mud and take in oxygen for the submerged roots
- D. They absorb water from the mud only at night

**Q844.** Many desert birds move long distances to areas where rain has recently fallen, because rainfall in these regions is unpredictable. This movement is best classified as
<!-- id: bio12a3-adapt-16 -->

- A. physiological
- B. structural
- C. reproductive
- D. behavioural

**Q845.** A seed-eating bird has a short, thick, cone-shaped beak. Which statement about this feature is correct?
<!-- id: bio12a3-adapt-17 -->

- A. It is a physiological adaptation that increases the bird's production of digestive enzymes
- B. It is a behavioural adaptation that makes the bird choose seeds over insects
- C. It is a structural adaptation that lets the bird crack and husk hard seeds
- D. It is a structural adaptation that stores water for use during dry periods

**Q846.** Which of the following is a physiological adaptation to cold?

- A. Shivering, which generates heat through rapid muscle contractions
- B. Sheltering in a shaded thicket during the hottest part of the day

- B. Salt glands near the eyes that secrete a concentrated salt solution
- C. Feeding only on prey that contains no salt

<!-- id: bio12a3-adapt-20 -->

- B. a structural adaptation that reduces its surface area
- C. a physiological adaptation that increases its tolerance of heat

**Q849.** During drought, many eucalypts close their stomata around midday to reduce water loss. This response is best classified as
<!-- id: bio12a3-adapt-21 -->

- C. physiological, because it is a regulated internal process

#### Adaptations to Australian environments

**Q860.** Many Australian plants, such as some Banksia species, have serotinous fruits that stay closed until exposed to heat. The main advantage of this adaptation is that
<!-- id: bio12a3-aus-1 -->

- A. seeds are protected from insect pollinators
- B. seeds are released after fire, when competition is reduced and nutrients from ash are available
- C. seeds can only germinate in very cold conditions
- D. the plant avoids being burnt in a fire

**Q861.** After bushfire, many eucalypts produce new shoots from buds protected beneath thick bark along their trunks. These are called
<!-- id: bio12a3-aus-2 -->

- A. lignotubers
- B. epicormic buds
- C. proteoid roots
- D. stolons

**Q862.** The spinifex hopping mouse can survive in the Australian desert without drinking water. Which combination of adaptations best explains this?
<!-- id: bio12a3-aus-3 -->

- A. It sweats heavily and drinks dew
- B. It produces very concentrated urine, obtains metabolic water from food and is nocturnal, staying in humid burrows during the day
- C. It has a large body and thick fur to retain water
- D. It hibernates through summer

<!-- id: bio12a3-aus-4 -->

- B. deep taproots that reach nitrogen in groundwater
- C. pneumatophores that take in oxygen
- D. leaves with very large surface areas

<!-- id: bio12a3-aus-5 -->

- B. structural
- C. physiological only
- D. an example of aestivation

<!-- id: bio12a3-aus-10 -->

<!-- id: bio12a3-aus-11 -->

- A. lungs

**Q871.** Many annual wildflowers in the arid interior survive dry years as dormant seeds, germinating only after heavy rain. The main advantage of this strategy is that
<!-- id: bio12a3-aus-12 -->

- A. seeds germinate in the dry season, when temperatures are lower and competition is lower
- B. seedlings germinate only when there is enough water to grow, flower and set seed before the soil dries out
- C. the adult plants store water in their seed coats, which then germinate when needed

**Q872.** Cushion plants in the alpine areas of the Australian Alps grow as low, dense mounds close to the ground. Compared with taller plants nearby, the cushion is most likely to be
<!-- id: bio12a3-aus-13 -->

- A. exposed to higher wind speeds, which increase its rate of photosynthesis
- B. more likely to lose water through its leaves because it is closer to the ground
- C. warmer and less windy, which reduces heat loss and water loss
- D. dispersed further from the parent plant by strong winds

**Q873.** Many leaves of Australian rainforest trees have a pointed tip, called a drip tip. The function of this tip is to
<!-- id: bio12a3-aus-14 -->

- A. trap water on the leaf so it can be absorbed through the surface
- B. increase the leaf's ability to absorb light in the shaded understorey
- C. close the stomata at the tip, reducing transpiration
- D. shed rainwater quickly, so the leaf dries and fungi and algae cannot grow on it

**Q874.** The Queensland lungfish (Neoceratodus forsteri) lives in slow, shallow waterholes that can become low in dissolved oxygen. It can survive these conditions because it
<!-- id: bio12a3-aus-15 -->

- A. gulps air at the surface and takes up oxygen through a single lung
- B. burrows into dry mud and stays dormant until the next wet season
- C. extracts oxygen from air through its gills while out of the water
- D. stores oxygen in its swim bladder for later use

**Q875.** Bearded dragons are reptiles that live in arid central Australia. Each morning they bask on rocks to warm their bodies before becoming active. Basking is best classified as

#### Interdependencies and keystone species

**Q892.** A keystone species is best defined as a species that
<!-- id: bio12a3-inter-1 -->

- A. is the most abundant species in an ecosystem
- B. has a disproportionately large effect on ecosystem structure relative to its abundance
- C. is always the top predator in a food web
- D. is found only in one ecosystem

**Q893.** Clownfish live among the stinging tentacles of sea anemones, gaining protection, while the anemone gains nutrients from clownfish waste and protection from some predators. This relationship is an example of
<!-- id: bio12a3-inter-2 -->

- A. parasitism
- B. commensalism
- C. mutualism
- D. amensalism

**Q894.** In south-eastern Australian kelp forests, removing a predator of sea urchins led to a large increase in sea urchin numbers and the loss of most kelp. This chain of effects is best described as
<!-- id: bio12a3-inter-3 -->

- A. a trophic cascade
- B. competitive exclusion
- C. primary succession
- D. biomagnification

**Q895.** Mistletoe attaches to eucalypt branches and extracts water and nutrients from the host. The relationship between mistletoe and its host is
<!-- id: bio12a3-inter-4 -->

- A. mutualism
- B. commensalism
- C. parasitism
- D. predation of a herbivore

**Q896.** Dingoes have been suggested as a keystone species in parts of arid Australia. In areas where dingoes are controlled, red fox and feral cat numbers increase and small native mammal numbers decline. Which explanation best fits these observations?

- A. Dingoes compete with small native mammals for food
- B. Dingoes suppress fox and cat numbers, reducing predation pressure on small native mammals
- C. Foxes and cats are prey of small native mammals
- D. Dingoes spread disease to foxes and cats only when their numbers are low

<!-- id: bio12a3-inter-9 -->

- A. parasitism
- B. commensalism
- C. mutualism
- D. competition

**Q901.** A eucalypt releases chemicals from its leaves that stop seeds of other plant species from germinating in the soil beneath it. The eucalypt gains nothing and is not affected by this. The relationship is an example of
<!-- id: bio12a3-inter-10 -->

- A. mutualism
- B. parasitism
- C. amensalism

**Q902.** Lichens are made of a fungus and an alga living closely together. The fungus supplies water and minerals, and the alga supplies sugars made by photosynthesis. Both partners gain from the relationship. This relationship is best described as
<!-- id: bio12a3-inter-11 -->

- A. parasitism
- B. commensalism
- C. competition
- D. mutualism

**Q903.** A fan-tailed cuckoo lays its eggs in the nest of a thornbill. The cuckoo chick is raised by the thornbill parents, who feed it at the expense of their own young. This relationship is best described as
<!-- id: bio12a3-inter-12 -->

- A. parasitism
- B. competition
- C. commensalism
- D. mutualism

**Q904.** A powerful owl hunts and eats ringtail possums, killing each one it catches. The relationship between the owl and the possum is best described as
<!-- id: bio12a3-inter-13 -->

- A. commensalism
- B. predation
- C. mutualism
- D. parasitism

**Q905.** Which statement best distinguishes an indicator species from a keystone species?
<!-- id: bio12a3-inter-14 -->

- A. An indicator species is always rare, while a keystone species is always common
- B. An indicator species is a predator, while a keystone species is a producer
- C. The presence or absence of an indicator species reflects environmental conditions, while a keystone species has a large effect on community structure relative to its abundance
- D. An indicator species can live in only one ecosystem, while a keystone species is found in many

#### Aboriginal and Torres Strait Islander ecological knowledge

**Q924.** Cultural burning practised by many Aboriginal communities typically involves
<!-- id: bio12a3-ik-1 -->

- A. large, intense fires lit in the hottest, driest part of summer
- B. small, cool, patchy fires lit at particular times of year, creating a mosaic of burnt and unburnt areas
- C. burning only after every tree in an area has died
- D. fires lit only to clear land permanently for agriculture

**Q925.** Which is the most likely ecological benefit of a mosaic of patches burnt at different times?
<!-- id: bio12a3-ik-2 -->

- A. It removes all habitat for small mammals
- B. It reduces fuel loads and provides refuges and a range of habitat ages for different species
- C. It prevents any plant from flowering
- D. It increases the likelihood of large, uncontrollable wildfires

**Q926.** Seasonal calendars developed by Aboriginal and Torres Strait Islander peoples, such as those of the Gulumoerrgin (Larrakia) people around Darwin, are based on
<!-- id: bio12a3-ik-3 -->

- B. detailed observation of local indicators such as weather, flowering of plants and animal behaviour
- C. the phases of the moon only
- D. randomly assigned periods of equal length

<!-- id: bio12a3-ik-4 -->

**Q932.** Traditional Ecological Knowledge held by Aboriginal and Torres Strait Islander peoples is best described as
<!-- id: bio12a3-ik-9 -->

- B. a set of general scientific principles developed in universities and then applied to Country
- C. knowledge limited to spiritual beliefs, with no practical ecological content
- D. a fixed list of plant and animal names recorded by early European explorers

**Q933.** Many savanna Traditional Owners burn early in the dry season rather than late in it, mainly because early, cooler burns
<!-- id: bio12a3-ik-10 -->

- A. produce the hottest fires, which clear the ground most thoroughly
- B. reduce the fuel that would otherwise feed larger, hotter late-season wildfires and their greenhouse gas emissions
- C. are the only type of fire that will spread through dry grass
- D. allow fire to spread into closed forest so that new growth appears

**Q934.** Some cycad seeds are toxic if eaten raw. Aboriginal peoples in northern Australia prepared them by soaking or leaching the seeds in water before eating them. The main purpose of this step was to
<!-- id: bio12a3-ik-11 -->

- A. ferment the sugars so the seeds taste sweeter
- B. soften the seed so it could be cracked more easily
- C. remove water-soluble toxins before the seed was eaten
- D. reduce the protein content so the seed would store for longer

**Q935.** An Indigenous Protected Area is best described as
<!-- id: bio12a3-ik-12 -->

- A. land that the federal government leases to Traditional Owners for grazing
- B. a national park in which all human use, including cultural burning, is banned
- C. a commercial fishing zone that Aboriginal people may use only for ceremonies
- D. land or sea country that Traditional Owners have voluntarily dedicated for conservation, managed under their own plans and knowledge

**Q936.** When gathering seed from a native grass or bush food, Traditional Owners often leave some of the seed on the plant. Why does this practice support sustainable use?
<!-- id: bio12a3-ik-13 -->

- A. the remaining seed allows the plant population to regenerate and still feeds wildlife
- B. plants cannot be harmed by any harvest, so the amount taken does not matter
- C. seed left on the plant always rots, so harvesting everything prevents waste
- D. harvested plants produce more seed each year because of the damage

**Q937.** In many Aboriginal kinship systems, particular plants and animals are linked to certain people, which can lead to
<!-- id: bio12a3-ik-14 -->

- A. a rule that every species must be eaten at least once each year
- B. obligations to care for those species, which can limit when and how much of them is taken
- C. the belief that species can be bought and sold within families
- D. the view that people should never intervene, because species always look after themselves

**Q938.** Some Aboriginal groups managed food plants such as yams not only by collecting them but by tending the plots. Which practice best illustrates this?
<!-- id: bio12a3-ik-15 -->

- A. digging up every tuber in a patch and leaving no plant material behind
- B. avoiding a patch entirely so that the plants are never disturbed
- C. replanting parts of the tuber and returning later, so the patch can supply food in future seasons
- D. importing non-native crops to replace the native yams

**Q939.** Many Australian plants have seeds or buds that respond to fire. How can a patchy, cool burn increase the abundance of such species?
<!-- id: bio12a3-ik-16 -->

- A. fire kills every seed in the soil, so the species must regrow only from roots
- B. fire makes the soil acidic, which these species require to grow
- C. fire permanently removes all competing plants, so the species never face competition again
- D. heat and smoke can trigger germination or resprouting, and reduced competition gives these species room to grow

**Q940.** Why do many Traditional Owners keep fire out of rainforest margins during burning?
<!-- id: bio12a3-ik-17 -->

- A. rainforest is fire-resistant and benefits from hot, intense fire
- B. hot fire can kill fire-sensitive rainforest plants and the animals that depend on them
- C. rainforest plants produce oils that make fire spread faster into the forest
- D. rainforest trees need fire to open their seed pods

**Q941.** In arid Australia, people can locate water by observing seed-eating birds such as finches and pigeons. Why is this an effective indicator?
<!-- id: bio12a3-ik-18 -->

- A. the birds store water in their crops and release it near the surface
- B. these birds must drink daily, so they fly to water in the early morning and late afternoon along predictable paths
- C. the birds only nest near water, so any nest reveals a nearby spring
- D. the birds feed only on water plants that grow in standing water

**Q942.** Traditional Owners can find the nest of a native stingless bee (sugarbag) in a hollow tree by
<!-- id: bio12a3-ik-19 -->

- A. listening for buzzing from inside tree trunks, mainly at night
- B. searching for the largest flowering eucalypt in the area and cutting into it
- C. watching the foraging bees and following their flight lines back to the hollow
- D. assuming the nest is in the closest tree to the nearest flower

**Q943.** A tracker wants to judge how recently an animal passed through sandy ground. Which evidence is most useful?
<!-- id: bio12a3-ik-20 -->

- A. whether the edges of the print are sharp or crumbled, and whether wind, rain or dew has altered it
- B. the exact length of the animal's stride, which shows its age
- C. the colour of the soil inside the print
- D. the total number of tracks in the area, since more tracks always mean a more recent visit

- D. Removing it has little effect, because other species quickly take its place

### Exam-style questions (mixed topics)

**Q965.** Glucose: membranes, insulin and diabetes
<!-- id: vce-bio12-x1 -->
*Multi-part question · 10 marks · Original question in the style of a Units 1 & 2 end-of-semester exam*

Glucose enters most body cells by facilitated diffusion through carrier proteins in the plasma membrane.

**a.** Explain why glucose needs a protein to cross the plasma membrane, but oxygen does not. *(2 marks)*

**b.** Explain why facilitated diffusion does not need energy from ATP. *(1 mark)*

**c.** Using the stimulus–response model, describe how blood glucose returns to its normal level after a meal. *(2 marks)*

**d.** Explain why this is an example of negative feedback. *(1 mark)*

**e.** In type 1 diabetes the immune system destroys the beta cells. Explain why, without treatment, blood glucose stays high after meals. *(2 marks)*

**f.** Insulin is a protein made inside beta cells and released into the blood. Name the organelles that make it, and describe how it leaves the cell. *(2 marks)*

**Q966.** Coat colour in guinea pigs
<!-- id: vce-bio12-x2 -->
*Multi-part question · 10 marks · Original question in the style of a Units 1 & 2 end-of-semester exam*

In guinea pigs, the allele for black coat (B) is dominant to the allele for white coat (b).

**a.** Give the genotype of a white guinea pig. *(1 mark)*

**b.** Two heterozygous black guinea pigs are crossed. Use a Punnett square to find the expected ratio of phenotypes in their offspring. *(2 marks)*

**c.** A breeder has a black guinea pig of unknown genotype. Describe a test cross to find its genotype, and the results that would show each genotype. *(2 marks)*

**d.** Explain two ways in which meiosis produces gametes that are genetically different from each other. *(2 marks)*

**e.** Guinea pig body cells contain 64 chromosomes. State the number of chromosomes in a guinea pig gamete. *(1 mark)*

**f.** A population of wild guinea pigs lives among dark volcanic rocks, and most of them are black. Explain how a black coat is a structural adaptation in this environment. *(2 marks)*

**Q967.** Cells that divide, specialise and die
<!-- id: vce-bio12-x3 -->
*Multi-part question · 10 marks · Original question in the style of a Units 1 & 2 end-of-semester exam*

Body cells divide by mitosis, while bacteria divide by binary fission.

**a.** Describe what happens during interphase. *(2 marks)*

**b.** List the stages of mitosis in order and describe what happens in metaphase. *(2 marks)*

**c.** Explain how apoptosis normally prevents tumours, and how a failure of apoptosis can lead to cancer. *(2 marks)*

**d.** Compare embryonic and adult stem cells. *(2 marks)*

**e.** A bacterium divides every 20 minutes. Starting with one cell, how many cells are there after 2 hours? *(1 mark)*

**f.** Explain why binary fission is simpler than mitosis. *(1 mark)*

**Q968.** Staying warm and staying hydrated
<!-- id: vce-bio12-x4 -->
*Multi-part question · 10 marks · Original question in the style of a Units 1 & 2 end-of-semester exam*

On a cold winter morning, a hiker sets out without a jacket.

**a.** Use the stimulus–response model to describe how the hiker's body responds to the cold. *(3 marks)*

**b.** Explain how vasoconstriction reduces heat loss. *(1 mark)*

**c.** A lizard basks on a warm rock in the morning. Explain why this is a behavioural adaptation for an ectotherm. *(2 marks)*

Later, after hiking for hours without drinking, the hiker's urine is small in volume and dark in colour.

**d.** Explain the role of antidiuretic hormone (ADH) in this response. *(2 marks)*

**e.** Describe two structural adaptations that help desert plants reduce water loss. *(2 marks)*

**Q969.** Challenge: running a marathon in the heat
<!-- id: vce-bio12-cx1 -->
*Challenge question*
*Multi-part question · 10 marks · Original challenge question in the style of a Units 1 & 2 end-of-semester exam*

Sam runs a marathon on a 32 °C day. His body temperature starts to rise, he sweats heavily, and he drinks little water during the race.

**a.** Using the stimulus–response model, identify the stimulus, receptor, control centre and effector involved in Sam's sweating. *(2 marks)*

**b.** Explain how vasodilation of skin arterioles helps Sam lose heat. *(2 marks)*

**c.** Sam loses a lot of water as sweat. Explain how his body reduces water loss in urine. *(3 marks)*

**d.** Late in the race Sam's blood glucose falls. Explain how his body responds. *(2 marks)*

**e.** Explain why the responses in parts b–d are examples of negative feedback. *(1 mark)*

**Q970.** Challenge: tracing an inherited condition
<!-- id: vce-bio12-cx2 -->
*Challenge question*
*Multi-part question · 8 marks · Original challenge question in the style of a Units 1 & 2 end-of-semester exam*

Anna and Ben are both unaffected, but their daughter Chloe has a rare inherited condition. Their son David is unaffected.

**a.** Explain how this information shows that the condition is recessive and is not X-linked recessive. *(2 marks)*

**b.** Give the genotypes of Anna and Ben (using A and a), and the probability that their next child is affected. *(2 marks)*

**c.** What is the probability that David, who is unaffected, is a carrier? *(1 mark)*

**d.** During which stage of meiosis do the A and a alleles separate? Explain. *(1 mark)*

**e.** Explain why a karyotype would not detect this condition. *(1 mark)*

**f.** A treatment greatly reduces Chloe's symptoms. Has her genotype changed? Explain. *(1 mark)*

<!-- id: bio34a3-spe-2 -->

- A. Geographical isolation → different selection pressures and genetic drift → genetic divergence → reproductive isolation
- B. Reproductive isolation → geographical isolation → gene flow → new species
- C. Mutation → gene flow → interbreeding → new species
- D. Polyploidy → hybridisation → geographical isolation → new species

<!-- id: bio34a3-spe-3 -->

- A. are the same species, as they can mate.
- B. are separate species, as their hybrid offspring are not fertile.
- C. are undergoing sympatric speciation right now.
- D. have no shared ancestor.

**Q1226.** Two populations of frogs live in the same pond, but one breeds in early spring and the other in late summer. This is an example of
<!-- id: bio34a3-spe-4 -->

- A. geographical isolation.
- B. gene flow.
- C. temporal (seasonal) reproductive isolation.
- D. hybrid sterility.

<!-- id: bio34a3-spe-5 -->

- B. polyploidy increases gene flow with the parent population.
- C. polyploid plants always have lower fitness and die out.
- D. a polyploid plant cannot produce fertile offspring with the parent species, so it is instantly reproductively isolated.

**Q1231.** Two frog populations can interbreed and produce fertile offspring in a laboratory but never do in the wild, because they breed at different times of year. This is:
<!-- id: vce-bio34-sp-c1 -->

- A. Postzygotic isolation
- B. Geographic isolation only

#### Evidence of relatedness and phylogenetic trees

**Q1241.** The forelimbs of a human, a bat and a whale have the same arrangement of bones but different functions. These are
<!-- id: bio34a3-erp-1 -->

- A. analogous structures, showing convergent evolution.
- B. vestigial structures, showing loss of function.
- C. homologous structures, showing divergent evolution from a common ancestor.
- D. analogous structures, showing a common ancestor.

**Q1242.** The wings of a bird and the wings of an insect are best described as
<!-- id: bio34a3-erp-2 -->

- A. homologous structures.
- B. analogous structures.
- C. vestigial structures.
- D. transitional structures.

<!-- id: bio34a3-erp-3 -->

- B. Kangaroos are more closely related to humans than to dogs.
- C. Humans share a more recent common ancestor with the rhesus monkey than with any of the other species listed.
- D. Dogs and kangaroos are the same species.

<!-- id: bio34a3-erp-4 -->

- A. a species that is now extinct with no descendants.
- B. a common ancestor from which two or more lineages diverged.
- C. the present-day species being compared.
- D. the number of mutations in a gene.

- B. Amino acid sequences never change over time.
- C. Proteins contain more information than genes.
- D. DNA comparisons detect all changes, including silent mutations and non-coding regions that do not alter amino acids.
